ParlaMint-ES_2015-05-13-CD150513.u269	Thank you very much, Minister.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-05-13-CD150513.u270	We are now discussing the draft law on volunteering and the amendments to the whole that have been tabled. The Minister for Health, Social Services and Equality has the floor for the presentation of the project on behalf of the Government.
ParlaMint-ES_2015-05-13-CD150513.u271	Thank you very much, Mr President. I rise again to the rostrum - as I said before - to present the draft law of the volunteer, whose processing has been joint throughout this future with that of the draft law of the third sector of social action that we have discussed before. It does not refer only to volunteering in the entities of the third sector of social action, but this law, unlike the previous one, refers to all fields of activity where the altruistic commitment of citizens is expressed. In this case, we are talking about more than six million volunteers in Spain. I think that this data can still be improved, but there are many people who contribute their time, their talent and also their vocation to improve the lives of others in very different fields that the law recognizes. The law brings cause that it has been two decades since the Law of Volunteering was passed in Spain in 1996, and logically the social reality in all that time has changed and has been enriched; there is a new dimension, we have all learned, and therefore I think it is a good time to give a new impetus to voluntary action through the updating of this law. In what concrete terms do we see that the reality that this law regulates has changed? We see that there is a new profile in volunteers in this year 2015, when we face this change. There is a greater presence of retired persons, pensioners and also younger persons or unemployed persons. There are new forms of action in volunteering that previously did not exist; in 1996 there was no such development of digital technology, of networks, nor was there the possibility of participation as that which is often done in a virtual way, and which is so effective. The world has changed very quickly and the laws also have to adapt to that change. There are new areas that previously were not as important or unrecognized and new actors and new spaces are also incorporated where people also find the possibility to launch themselves into voluntary activity. And the law also seeks to involve both companies and universities. In short, there are new and different motivations, there are new schemes of participation, and the law has to collect all those possibilities that have been opened up. Again I want to say that this is a law that I believe you will speak of in principle well, despite having tabled your amendments in full on this frequent and unfounded accusation to the government that it invades areas of competition. In any case, I hope that you will speak well of a law that also arises from a very fluid dialogue with the entities of the third sector themselves and with the same autonomous communities, with which we have naturally been talking and reaching consensus in different areas. The law, Mr President, has five objectives: to regulate the essential core of voluntary action in its various fields and possibilities of participation; to collect and establish the legal framework for voluntary entities; to enhance volunteering throughout life, regulating in a separate and distinct way the rights and duties of voluntary persons, and also the rights and duties of persons receiving voluntary action. The fourth objective is to establish the framework for cooperation with public administrations, and finally, as I told them to encourage, to recognise voluntary action by bringing in new actors who already volunteer, such as companies and universities. In this regard, therefore, there are numerous developments in the draft law. The legal regime of voluntary persons and the relationship they have to maintain with the entities are established, other areas of volunteering are envisaged, and account is taken not only of the activity taking place on national territory, but also of that which can be done outside our borders. What's volunteering? According to the definition of the law, it is those activities of general interest carried out by people who have a solidarity vocation, who carry out it freely without financial and material benefit, channeling this action through the accredited entities. Therefore, other isolated actions outside the entities, which are carried out for family or friendly reasons or by virtue of a labour or commercial relationship, are not considered voluntary. This is not considered within the scope of volunteering, nor are scholarships, non-employment practices in enterprises or external academic practices. The law wants to be very careful in determining this distinction from volunteering as an unselfish action that does not interfere with other paid activity or work activity. In fact, it expressly states that volunteering can never be a cause of termination of a contract or a decrease in a service, it cannot be. I therefore believe that voluntary action and voluntary persons are well limited and protected. From there, the law establishes the values and principles on which voluntary action is based, namely freedom, participation, solidarity, citizen co-responsibility, complementarity, autonomy, gratuitousness, efficiency, equal treatment, non-discrimination, and accessibility, detailing and expressing in the law the new areas of current volunteering. These come to be, in addition to social volunteering - which we have been talking about all afternoon - development cooperation, environmental, cultural, sporting, leisure and leisure, community and civil protection. It is a vast field of participation where people lend their time in favor of the general interest. Other issues have been introduced, such as paralympic volunteering within the category of sport volunteering, or animal protection within the category of environmental volunteering. He spoke earlier about the new forms of volunteerism exercised through information and communication technologies. I have mentioned that the status of the rights and duties of volunteers and persons to whom voluntary action is addressed is laid down. Volunteers have - and is collected here -, among other rights, that of receiving basic and specialized training for the development of their action within the entity, the possibility to participate actively in the organization of volunteering, and the right to pay for the repayable expenses incurred in carrying out their activity. Its duties include rejecting any economic compensation, respecting the confidentiality and rights of the persons benefiting from its action, and also the duty to participate in the training sections. Equality in access to volunteer status for dependent persons, including persons with disabilities, and older persons, has been sought and is intended to be given priority by law, so that voluntary action can be adapted to appropriate formats and conditions in accordance with their circumstances and personal capacity. There is something new that some of you have doubts about and that we can discuss, but it is also expressly mentioned the volunteering of minors since the age of twelve, as a way, and it seems to us important in the learning of solidarity and in the development of a commitment to the environment, usually under the decision of parents and guardians, as it could not be otherwise. But voluntary action, civic engagement, that social participation that we used to invoke in the previous debate, I think can and should be learned from an early age, and we are learning about volunteering experiences, even family volunteering, from even earlier ages that are enriching for all those who participate in them and who educate young people in that solidarity. We have also introduced restrictions. We have introduced restrictions on certain persons who for certain conduct cannot participate in voluntary action. We do so for the protection, primarily for the protection of minors, for the protection of entities as well. But the law introduces a ban, and that is that in programmes aimed at minors, persons sentenced to a final sentence for offences against the sexual freedom, trafficking and exploitation of minors cannot be integrated. You know that in the child protection laws that are now in process - and that if I don't get very involved in the rostrum you will be able to discuss this afternoon, when the plenary is over - we have already introduced, among other things, the development of the pederast register in accordance with the Lanzarote agreement. With this tool, I believe that we can also ensure that those who have been convicted of this type of crime are not related to minors in the exercise of voluntary action. And we, while respecting how it cannot be otherwise, the autonomy of social action entities have also included caution in the cases of people with a history not cancelled for crimes of gender violence, who are not going to be able to be part of programs that involve contact with families, women or young people. I think it is also a reasonable and protective precaution. Nor are people convicted of illegal trafficking in persons, that is, trafficking, or terrorism. These are the offences in which there is a limitation on participation, always taking into account, of course, beyond the prohibition, benefits and reintegration work, which are also contained in voluntary programmes; but this is naturally compatible with those prohibitions and with those cautions. As regards the compatibility of voluntary action with work, the promotion of measures to reduce or adapt working hours, always in accordance with collective bargaining, is expanded. In this sense, too, the Administration, with its own workers, will encourage them to adapt or reduce their hours, and this will allow them to participate in volunteering activities, provided of course that they have nothing to do with the replacement of the public services that the administrations are obliged to provide. I have already said that companies and universities are new actors in volunteering. Companies will be able to do so whenever their actions are adapted to the general interest, and their workers can join if they wish to do so without having to affect their contract at all, and of course voluntary action will also be carried out through voluntary bodies, there are many companies that are already doing this and we want to encourage it, and I think it is also good from the point of view of commitment and the exercise of corporate social responsibility in companies. And universities will be able to support and promote voluntary actions among the university population in the areas of awareness-raising, training or research. It calls for the development of intermediation between students and voluntary entities. In this sense, we want the centres to be able to establish academic recognition formulas for volunteering actions when the necessary academic requirements are met, and the values and principles set out in this law are respected. I therefore believe that we are looking for a development of positive aspects, of regulation, of the protection of volunteers; where we also set out the relationship that the volunteer has with the entity where he or she is going to provide his or her services, what are the rights, the limitations, the duties that he or she has with regard to himself or herself, also with regard to the person who is the beneficiary of his or her contribution, and in this sense we create a framework that is modern, that is up-to-date, and that greatly improves the regulation that we had before. I insist that no competition is invaded, neither wants nor wants to invade any competition, seeks to give a general regulation, seeks to improve a law. A great deal has been done with all the autonomous communities to prevent the emergence - I see emerging - of fears in this regard. It seems to me that by having this renewed tool of work for the entities that volunteer and for the Spanish volunteers, now that we have that data of six million volunteers that makes us the sixth country of the European Union with the largest number of volunteers, we can still make a leap forward and give more prominence to the exercise of that commitment by the citizens, and that in the debate of this law, and in the capacity of agreement that we can deploy parliamentary groups and from the government in this House, this constitutes a recognition of the need in Spain for civil society to be stronger, more participatory and more demanding with the public authorities. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2015-05-13-CD150513.u279	Thank you, Mr. President. In any case, from La Izquierda Plural we raise questions and therefore we present our amendment to the whole, because we doubt the adequacy and the timeliness of the bill submitted by the Government. At the outset, it must be recognized that the citizens of our country, in an organized or individual manner, play an increasingly important role in the design and implementation of actions aimed at the satisfaction of the general interest and the construction of a solidarity society. Volunteering is the expression of the solidarity and unselfishness of this citizenship, which increasingly demands participation. It should therefore also be recognized that the Voluntary Act of 1996 has been clearly overtaken. Today it has changed the profile of volunteers, the ways to collaborate, the actors that promote voluntary actions, motivations and up to the time and form that citizenship dedicates to social entities. The new law should carry out an update of the reality of voluntary action through a new indispensable legal framework, recognizing the contribution and development of the autonomous communities to volunteering in our country. It must be borne in mind that legislation affects - as you said, Minister, earlier - 6 million people in our country, that Spain is the sixth country of the European Union in terms of the participation of citizens in volunteering activities, and therefore any regulatory development must have the consensus of the entities and respect the essence of what volunteering means. It is therefore a project with inadequacies, without social or political consensus, ambiguous in the definition of the functions of volunteering and the generation and occupation of jobs, in addition to the possible usurpation of the functions of volunteering by enterprises and public administrations and the failure to meet the objective of reintegration set out in many of the public policies, sufficient reasons to present this amendment as a whole. As I said in the debate on the previous point, we hope that we can contribute with the partial amendments to improve the text and reach agreements, if possible. Thank you very much, Mr President. ParlaMint-ES_2015-05-13-CD150513.u281	Thank you very much, Mr Pérez Moya. To present his amendment, Mr Campuzano has the floor for the Catalan Group. [[Convergence and Union]]
ParlaMint-ES_2015-05-13-CD150513.u282	Thank you, Mr. President. Minister, our group is also tabling an amendment in its entirety. Here the reasons are complementary to those of the previous draft law but also different. We share the criticism that other groups have made about the competitive invasion, in which we will then go into depth because in this case, unlike the law of the third sector, the determination of a basic character through the appeal to article 149.1.1, in our view, de facto produces an intervention in the competences of the autonomous parliaments in this matter so far, in particular in the case of our status of autonomy through article 166. In addition, it seems to us to be an excessively interventionist, detailed and regulatory text in a matter where the important thing is to give freedom to individuals and entities once a minimum legal framework is guaranteed. The model of volunteering enshrined in the law departs at least from the majority perception in Catalonia of how volunteering should be articulated. Finally, we do not have the perception - I hope the debate serves to clarify this - that the law ends by resolving any of the problems that may exist in relation to the current regulation. And there, Minister, I come to a point. Some spokesmen have insisted - and yourself in your intervention - on the need to modernize the legal framework of 1996, but if you observe that law you will see two things that are not minor. It has been a law that in terms of the competences of the autonomous communities has not created any problems. It was very clear that the law affected activities of a supra-autonomous nature and whether there was any voluntary activity in the sphere of the exclusive policies of the State there was no conflict with the autonomous communities; it had been peaceful during all these years and had allowed anyone who wished to develop their own laws and policies without any problem. There are 16 articles that are rather short and scarce but that guarantee a minimum framework for resolving an issue that was not resolved until then and that addressed the issue of ensuring the activity of volunteering. Faced with this, we are now faced with a bill, I insist, which is extremely detailed in matters that I do not know whether a law of the State should be able to regulate. For example, should the minimum content of the volunteering programme be regulated by a law of the State? Minister, in Catalonia, a law on volunteering is now being processed with a certain degree of controversy on the part of the social sectors affected by this legislation. At the beginning of the 1990s, the Government of the Generalitat chose to do two things; one, to create an instrument of public policy, the Catalan Institute of Volunteering - some of the spokespersons have said - because it seemed to us that, rather than regular, we should accompany through public policies; and two, regulation, the International Charter of Volunteering. Since 1991, volunteering in Catalonia has grown, developed and operated without the need for a law to regulate it. Now here they bring a dense law that also threatens further regulatory developments; a law made from the logic, sometimes well-intentioned, of government bureaucracy that believes that social problems are solved through regulation or that citizens' initiative needs a legal framework as restrictive as it may end up being. A first consideration, therefore, was it necessary to present an alternative to the 1996 law, or was it necessary, on the 1996 law, to make some of the adjustments that could have been made in these twenty years? The second consideration is the competitive invasion. The 1996 law did not refer to article 149.1.1; it did not, therefore, introduce the claim to establish a common model equally assumed by all the autonomous communities, but that is what this law seeks. The 1996 Act did not seek to create a common model, but rather a legal framework for the development of voluntary activity at the state level. If the aim is to equalize the model of volunteering, we disagree, among other issues, because Article 166 of the Statute of Autonomy of Catalonia establishes exclusive competence. Before that, Mr Tardà and I reaffirmed myself in that argument, the constitutional model is not the one in which the State defines the policies and the autonomous communities develop them; it is not that, because then the concept of self-government is empty of political content. The State can create a framework within which it is competent and should regulate, but it must not aspire to establish a common model. Why? If it turns out that Catalan society and the Catalan Parliament want another model of volunteering and have exclusive competence, can they not develop it, Minister? Moreover, if, through the provisions of article 17 (e) of the draft law, subsidies of 0.7 that affect Catalan entities must also be adapted to the common model, we are, in fact, in a process of refocusing this policy, of uniformity, rather than opting to give freedom for the autonomous communities to develop their own models. We're talking about law enforcement. The spokesman for the People's Group, Mrs Vázquez, speaking of the 0.7 management, said that no matter who distributes the money. My mother would be fine to say so, but you are a Member of Parliament and know that there is a competition framework set out in the Statutes of Autonomy and in the Constitution, and there is also a ruling of the Constitutional Court that states that in matters that fall within the competence of the autonomous communities the State can launch subsidies, but the management must be autonomous. That is what the law and the rulings of the Constitutional Court say, and that is what we are calling for from this rostrum. Therefore, in this case we are concerned. Some spokesman has quoted - I believe it has been the spokesman of the Basque Group - that statement contained in the law: Voluntary persons who participate in State-level programmes or who exceed the territory of an autonomous community will be subject to this law. There we may have some confusion between state regulation and autonomous regulation, unless you pretend that the state is the one that the autonomous communities should then replicate, when perhaps it would be reasonable for state legislation to have the capacity to regulate supra-autonomic programmes, as it did so far in the 1996 Act, and, if you wish, to have an extra character in what the autonomous legislation could not contemplate. Therefore, there is a fundamental reason for criticism. Some spokesmen who have spoken have made this clear. The philosophy of the draft law, together with that interventionism that I told you before, raises issues that at least a part of Catalan civil society looks at with some mistrust; for example, volunteering linked to the public sector, to administrations, an issue that is always delicate, very tempting for local administrations, and with the risk that volunteering, through public administrations, will enter the field of hiring workers. This generates mistrust in Catalan civil society, among other issues because the model of volunteering that we have sought to promote in Catalonia is very much based on the initiative of society, on the initiative of NGOs, on the initiative of entities, on entities and citizens, not through volunteering directly linked to administrations. Even recognizing the value it has in terms of promoting corporate social responsibility policies and, therefore, policies of companies that favour their employees being involved, the formula as regulated in the draft law causes some concern in part of a segment of civil society about the risk of commercializing volunteering or eventually turning it into activities very marquetinian but far removed from the main virtue of volunteering, which is not only the citizen's involvement in what is of general interest, the citizen's involvement in what is common to all, but also in strengthening the associative fabric. It is interesting the volunteering of those companies that involve their employees in civil society initiatives and is interested when alliances between NGOs and companies occur; there we have very suggestive and very interesting experiences. It is so interesting and so suggestive, at least seen with the model that in Catalonia generated a certain consensus, that companies generate their own volunteering within their company, minister, that the model underlying the bill does not end up linking with the look we have had since Catalonia in this matter. In this bill, if the real minister wishes, not only with the speech from the rostrum or in the reply, to be able to resolve the issues that concern us, there is room. Let us return to the competitive model defined in the 1996 Act, which has been peaceful, I do not have the impression that it has created any kind of difficulty; it has allowed, in what the State was competent to do, to be able to carry out its activity, to function, therefore, it would not make sense for the draft law to contain any reference to 149.1.1. In 1996 it was not present and that allowed such regulation, so let us leave it with that basic character that you have introduced, but let us give it, yes - the 1996 Act did not formulate it - a supplementary character; a supplementary state framework that effectively complements what the autonomous communities are developing makes sense, or even for that autonomous community that does not have legislative development in this matter that the reference law can be the law that the State makes, and we thin out the normative content of the bill. Mr Guillaumes will agree more than ever with me: let us be more liberal in the legislative approach of this initiative, let us not rule so much, because in volunteering we do not need to regulate so much, among other things because, fortunately, reality always exceeds the invention capacity of the regulator. Are types of volunteering defined? For in two years' time there will be issues that are not to be defined in this law. Is that going to be relevant? Probably not. On this issue of Public Administration, corporate volunteering or a question that has appeared in different interventions and that has much to do with the reintegration of people who have gone out of prison, let us be able to stop a generous look as the social initiative in Catalonia has, which continues to believe that these people need a second chance and that precisely by participating in volunteering programs they can have that second chance. Mr Minister, there are elements here that would allow us to reach a desirable consensus, because in the same way as in the previous bill we were sorry to have to submit an amendment to the whole, we are also sorry to have to submit an amendment to the whole of this bill. In the praise we all give of volunteering, we must emphasize the importance of social capital in the successful functioning of democracy and in the successful functioning of societies. Those societies that between political power, public power and individuals have intermediate institutions that facilitate the engagement of citizens in what is of all, in what is common, in what has to do with the general interest, are better societies, more democratic societies, more prosperous, more free. On this fundamental issue, as a Catalan MEP, I would like to point out that there is full agreement that what has always defined Catalan society is that voluntary commitment expressed in a multitude of initiatives, most of which have nothing to do with the Government of Catalonia or with municipal governments, but simply with the initiative of its citizens. That's what we're working on and that's what we're working on. Thank you very much. [[Applauds]]

ParlaMint-ES_2015-05-13-CD150513.u286	Thank you, Mr. President. Ladies and gentlemen, Minister, the draft law on volunteerism comes to this Chamber, a law which unfortunately does not have the support of civil society and which the political groups see for the first time in this debate. We hope that at the end of the process that we begin today we will be able to pass among all a law that has at least the same support as obtained by Law 6/1996 of 15 January of the Volunteership, the current law. I therefore begin my intervention to stress the necessary and indispensable task of reaching consensus that allows us to have a law that is in line with the needs of the sector and that also encourages the participation of citizens in the construction of a plural, democratic, just and free society. They are progressing that my group will work towards maximum consensus. An example of this is the failure on our part to introduce an amendment to the whole, which under no circumstances should be understood or interpreted as making this bill that the Government is bringing to us today seem right to us. During the period of amendments, we will make the improvements that we consider essential for the text to improve and we will also provide effective work to understand the challenges facing the sector. The 1978 Constitution, in its article 1, clearly determines the unequivocal will of our society: 'Spain is constituted in a social and democratic State governed by law, which promotes freedom, justice, equality and political pluralism as the highest values of its legal system'. The work of volunteering enables our society to advance and strengthen a fundamental pillar of our democracy: a social and democratic state of law. In the face of individualism - no longer only of capitalist but also social court - where the confrontation of absolute truths leads to the division of society, volunteering allows and advocates a just and equal society thanks to its creative and mediating action. The participation of civil society through its voluntary action magnifies the democratic values of our rule of law. A democracy achieves its highest value through participation not only as a democratic right of choice for political representatives, but also as an expression of active and dynamic citizenship. Article 9.2 of the 1978 Constitution determines the promotional role of public authorities in removing obstacles to or hindering the participation of citizens. This role of the State is complemented by the expression of solidarity and unselfishness of voluntary action, thus enriching the values of our democratic State of law. Let me highlight what we have built as a society. Since January 15, 1996, when this same House passed the first Volunteer Law - by the way, the second law in Europe, behind the Italian one - we have made much progress as a country and as a society. That approval was a great advance for the voluntary movement in Spain. From that moment on we have seen how different laws have been passed in each of the autonomous communities, a clear reflection of the unequivocal commitment of Spanish society for the strengthening of voluntary action. During this period, four State volunteering plans have been approved, in addition to the specific plans of the different regions of Spain; seventeen State voluntary congresses have been held and a significant number of voluntary entities and individuals have been distinguished through the social volunteering awards. In short, the 1996 law has been an essential element of the momentum of the voluntary movement in our country. There is no doubt that there is a broad consensus about the desirability, opportunity and need to work towards strengthening volunteering in Spain, which allows us to state that the commitment we launched in 1996 to promote and develop volunteering has served to boost social awareness of the irreplaceable role of volunteering. According to the 2011 CIS data, there are about 12 million volunteers in Spain - that is, 31% of the people have taken voluntary actions on some occasion - of which about 6.5 million - as the Minister said, 17% of the population - have been volunteers in the same year. This leaves us still far from Europe's average, which puts the eurozone at 30%. This is undoubtedly one of the challenges that we need to take into account in the new law that begins today's debate. As for the role of volunteering, it should be noted that 46.5 % of volunteering devotes its activity to social action, marking a clear trend in our country towards improving the quality of life of people. If we look at age, we see that there is a high dispersion, turning our country's voluntary action into a multigenerational element. But we must not settle for what has been achieved so far, but we must go further. There is a need to improve knowledge and information on volunteering so as to further increase the number of people joining voluntary action. As was already done in 2011, which was declared the European Year of Voluntary Activities, we must take advantage of the processing of this bill to put on the political and social agenda the relevance of voluntary action. In addition, we must reflect on new forms of volunteering as an expression of the solidarity of citizens and not as a social instrument for replacing the actions that the Government is no longer carrying out in the social sphere. Volunteering, as my colleague Mrs Pérez has rightly said, cannot become a substitute for the Government's discharge of functions. Volunteering is a basic expression of human relations. The participation of citizens in social life enhances the fundamental character of our rule of law. Democracy must be built with the work of all, the impulse of public institutions and the participation of citizens through voluntary action. Without participation there can be no democracy. At this time of economic crisis and cuts in rights and freedoms perpetrated by the Government of the People's Party and growing individualism, a social impulse for democracy is more necessary, and this is closely linked to improving the participation of citizens. Volunteering gives our society a more open, fair and equal profile: a broad-spectrum democracy. Unfortunately, the introduction of this bill has not started well, as today we are discussing four amendments in their entirety. Moreover, it is a law that does not even come here with the consensus of the voluntary sector. A bad performance, Minister. It is not right to agree on a draft with organised civil society and then to present and approve in the Council of Ministers another draft other than that which was agreed upon. I have already announced to you at the beginning of my speech that our will is to offer agreements that will improve this law, but such actions are not a good letter of introduction. Today's bill has ideological biases that have nothing to do with the needs of the voluntary sector I have presented to you. We understand - and we have demanded it on many occasions - that almost twenty years after the previous law it was necessary to adapt it to the current reality, but, yes, the law cannot serve to divide between first and second volunteers; nor to draw a very fine line between young and selfless volunteering; not even, as is done in the memory of the analysis of the normative impact of the project, to quantify economically the action of volunteering by equipping it with jobs. I'm on my way. The action of volunteering cannot be summed up in an economic value of saving public arks. As I mentioned earlier, volunteering is not to replace the evil done by his Government. As the Economic and Social Council has stated, it is in the principle of solidarity participation that volunteerism finds its primary foundation, not in what you call co-responsibility with society. This law must serve as a new impetus; indeed, a social, political and normative, but also an economic one. Floral games are not worth the toast to the sun. To bet on volunteering is also to put this momentum in the General Budgets of the State. If not, you will be doing a weak favor for the promotion of volunteering and for all those who dedicate part of their time in an unselfish way to other people and other social or environmental purposes. We must take advantage of this moment to improve the regulations related to volunteering, to promote policies to promote volunteering, to put on the political, social and media agenda the work of volunteering in Spain and, of course, to improve the instruments that facilitate this impulse. From the Socialist Parliamentary Group we are going to work in this direction. My partner Martín González and the one who subscribes, as rapporteurs of the law, we will listen to the sector and to all those people who want to bring ideas that will improve the project. We will work to have a law participated by the citizenry, and thanks to the collaboration of the platform Quórum porto org. we have opened through the Internet a permanent channel of communication and participation of citizens. We bet on an expansive democracy that allows our representative democracy to be completed with a participatory democracy. I'm done. Our position is clear. This law cannot lead to a retrogression in the right of all persons, as provided for in the Constitution, to participate freely in voluntary activities. Nor can it be a law to confuse the spirit and role of volunteers and volunteers and, of course, this law cannot represent the great regulatory impetus that the sector needs to continue to grow. ParlaMint-ES_2015-05-13-CD150513.u290	Thank you, Mr. President. I appeal to your political responsibility to maintain the spirit of dialogue and consensus with which you were born during the rest of the parliamentary process. Ladies and gentlemen, returning to what we are dealing with, the draft law on volunteering, I would like to stress that it is part of the ambitious social agenda undertaken by the Government, which, through instruments such as the Youth Strategy 2020, the Action Plan for the Spanish Disability Strategy, the bill for the third sector of social action, which we have just discussed, and the next comprehensive plan to support the family, aims to consolidate economic growth and strengthen the welfare state. The draft law on volunteering that is being brought to this House today is an example of coordination and modernization, since, in addition to demarcating with greater parity the competition margins of public administrations, it addresses the changes that have taken place since the enactment of the law in force in 1996, through modern drafting. In addition, this bill, as the Minister has already pointed out, defines the concept of volunteering in positive terms; establishes the legal regime for voluntary persons and voluntary entities; includes the values and principles underlying voluntary action; regulates volunteering in all areas; promotes and recognizes voluntary action, bringing in new actors, such as companies and universities, and introduces certain restrictions on voluntary participation. This limits voluntary action to persons sentenced to a final sentence for offences against sexual freedom, for offences of trafficking and for the exploitation of minors, including those convicted of crimes of gender-based violence or domestic violence and those convicted of illegal trafficking in persons and/or terrorism. In short, ladies and gentlemen, we are faced with a new law that seeks to respond to the 6 million Spanish volunteers, a new law for the exercise of solidarity, without adjectives, in accordance with the current social reality; a new law aimed at promoting the general interest, the common good, the solidarity deployment of human capacities, the construction of equity, justice and social cohesion; a new law for the effort of social participation and the promotion of solidarity initiatives, also among the smallest, the elderly and persons with disabilities; a new law open to the promotion of volunteerism, also from the company, the administration and the university; a new law with a state regulation respectful of the areas of competence of the public administrations, with a common core essential that promotes collaboration with the third sector to promote solidarity action and seek the greater commitment of the administrations. With regard to the four amendments to the whole tabled by La Izquierda Plural, Convergence i Unió, Grupo Vasco and Grupo Mixto, Esquerra Republicana de Catalunya, I would like to say that the bill represents a competitive invasion, which does not break down or interfere with the competences of the autonomous communities. Moreover, it is a regulatory framework that respects the scenario of shared competences set out by the Constitutional Court in its judgement 31/2010 of 28 June, while at the same time declaring that the assumption of competences by the autonomous communities in the field of volunteering is respectful of the constitutional order of competences. [[The lady]] Thus, a constitutional perspective does not prevent the exercise of the powers of the State when these compete with the autonomous over the same physical space, that is, over the same legal object. The law will therefore apply without prejudice to the powers of the Autonomous Communities. In addition, the new wording of article 2 seeks to be consistent with the doctrine of the High Court and should be read and interpreted in conjunction with the first additional provision and articles 17.2 and 18.1. Secondly, with regard to volunteering under the age of sixteen, article 5 of the present draft law recognizes the existence of dimensions where it is possible, with the necessary adaptations, to involve adults, children and persons over the age of twelve and under the age of sixteen. In addition, these minors may participate in voluntary initiatives only if they are expressly authorized by their parents, guardians or legal representatives, provided that they do not harm their development or comprehensive training. Thirdly, as regards the limits on access to volunteering, the Catalan Parliamentary Group makes a general statement which is inaccurate, since it does not reproduce the different assumptions referred to in the draft law. In this connection, it is necessary to specify that the first reference is made to crimes committed against freedom and sexual compensation, trafficking and exploitation of minors, where incorporation is almost absolutely impossible or, where appropriate, the suspension of voluntary participation with minors is justified. In the case of other offences, account shall be taken of whether or not they are cancelled, when the voluntary entity selects or, where appropriate, suspends the voluntary person. Fourthly, as regards the reduction of civil liability, the wording of article 10 and, in particular, of 14.3, it cannot be deduced that the level of requirement of civil liability has been lowered. Fifthly, the involvement of business and public administrations in the promotion and promotion of volunteering involves the creation of a sort of new volunteer. Article 21 of the promotion of volunteering in the company sets precise limits on entrepreneurial activity: on the one hand, promotion or, where appropriate, participation in volunteering programmes is subject to actions which may be classified as of general interest, are included in one of the areas of voluntary action and respect the values and principles underlying voluntary action; on the other hand, they must have the cooperation of voluntary entities, even if only for reasons of effectiveness of voluntary action. Sixthly, with regard to the fact that companies will find a whole list of possibilities to perform voluntary activities within their working hours, it should be said that Article 9(1) of the draft law provides that female workers and female employees and public employees may only engage in voluntary activities outside their working hours. Seventhly and lastly, as regards economic viability, I would like to point out that the limit on the increase in expenditure is justified, among other reasons, by the fact that the planned creation of the two bodies, the Interministerial Commission on Volunteering and the State Observatory on Volunteering, is merely a recognition of what already exists. Furthermore, no reference is being made at any time to the reduction or suspension of resources currently provided for volunteering. Ladies and gentlemen, for all that I have said, my group will vote against all the amendments tabled, but I repeat that I hope that the spirit of dialogue and consensus with which it was born will be maintained during the parliamentary process. I conclude, Mr President, by expressing the commitment of the People's Parliamentary Group to the work of the Spanish volunteers from the awareness of the important role they play in our society. We know that volunteer action has been built up as a key piece for recovery and has become a restorative balm for many citizens. That is why we believe that the work carried out by these people is commendable work, which deserves the support and support of all groups and political powers. Minister, I cannot leave this rostrum without congratulating you and congratulating you on this bill that the former Minister Mato initiated and which you have completed in an absolutely extraordinary way. My congratulations, Minister, and those of the People's Parliamentary Group. Thank you, Madam President. [[Applauds]]
